=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is an integer sequence x = (x_1, \ldots, x_N), which is initialized with x_1 = \cdots = x_N = 0.
You will perform M operations on this integer sequence. In the i-th operation, you are given an integer pair (L_i, R_i) such that 1 \leq L_i \leq R_i \leq N, and you must perform exactly one of the following three operations:

- Operation 0: Do nothing. This operation incurs a cost of 0.
- Operation 1: For each integer j with 1 \leq j \leq N, if L_i \leq j \leq R_i holds, set x_j = 1. This operation incurs a cost of 1.
- Operation 2: For each integer j with 1 \leq j \leq N, if L_i \leq j \leq R_i does not hold, set x_j = 1. This operation incurs a cost of 1.

Your goal is to make x_1 = \cdots = x_N = 1 hold at the end. Determine whether this goal can be achieved. If it can be achieved, present one way to achieve it where the total cost of the operations is minimized.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
L_1 R_1
\vdots
L_M R_M

Output

If the goal is not achievable, print -1.
If the goal is achievable, print one way to achieve it where the total cost of the operations is minimized, in the following format, where K is the minimum total cost of the operations, and \mathrm{op}_i is the type of operation (0, 1, or 2) chosen for the i-th operation.
K
\mathrm{op}_1 \cdots \mathrm{op}_M

If there are multiple ways that minimize the total cost, printing any one of them is accepted.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 1000000
- 1 \leq M \leq 200000
- 1 \leq L_i \leq R_i \leq N
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 4
2 4
3 5
1 4
2 5

Sample Output 1

2
2 0 1 0

In the sample output, x changes as follows:

- Initially, x = (0,0,0,0,0).
- In the 1st operation, Operation 2 is performed. x_1 and x_5 become 1, so x = (1,0,0,0,1).
- In the 2nd operation, Operation 0 is performed. x remains (1,0,0,0,1).
- In the 3rd operation, Operation 1 is performed. x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4 become 1, so x = (1,1,1,1,1).
- In the 4th operation, Operation 0 is performed. x remains (1,1,1,1,1).

Sample Input 2

5 4
1 3
1 5
2 4
3 5

Sample Output 2

1
0 1 0 0

Sample Input 3

5 2
1 3
2 5

Sample Output 3

2
1 1

Sample Input 4

5 2
1 3
2 4

Sample Output 4

-1

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's define interval cover problem: Given a set of intervals S (available), target [l,r], min number of intervals from S whose union covers [l,r]. If target empty, cost 0. If impossible, INF. Standard greedy: sort intervals by L ascending; start pos=l; among intervals with L ≤ pos, choose one with max R; pos=maxR+1; count. Need target fully covered. Intervals can extend beyond target. Complexity O(k log k) per query. Need many queries.

We need compute:
c0 = cover(U) using all intervals.
c1 = min_i 1 + cover_excluding_i(I_i).
c2 = min_{i<j} 2 + cover_excluding_{i,j}(I_i∩I_j), with empty intersection cost 0.

Efficient detection:

N up to 1e6, M up to 2e5.

Disjoint pair:
Sort intervals by L. Sweep maintain max R seen so far among previous intervals. For each interval in sorted by L, if max_R_before < L_i, then previous interval with max_R_before is disjoint (ends before current starts). Since sorted by L, if any previous R < L_i, disjoint. We can find pair. Also if current R < previous L? Sorting by L handles previous L≤current L, so disjoint if previous R < current L. If intervals with same L, not disjoint unless R<L impossible. So sweep works.
Need store original indices.

Containment pair:
Need find i≠j with L_i ≤ L_j and R_i ≥ R_j. Sort by L ascending, and for equal L by R descending? Standard: sort by L ascending, R descending. Sweep maintain maximum R seen so far. For current interval j, if there exists previous interval i with L_i ≤ L_j and R_i ≥ R_j, then containment. Since sorted by L, previous L≤current L. Need previous R≥current R. Maintain max R among previous. If max_R >= R_j, then the interval with max_R contains current. But careful: if max_R comes from an interval with same L and R maybe; okay. If max_R >= R_j, choose that previous interval as outer, current as inner. If equal intervals, sorted by R descending, first equal has R equal, contains current. Good.
But what if containment is current interval contains previous? Sorting by L ascending, previous L≤current L, so previous cannot be contained in current unless L equal and R? Actually if current contains previous, then current L≤prev L, so current would come before previous in sorted order (if L smaller) or same L with larger R. Thus it will be detected when previous (the inner) is processed after outer. So one sweep detects any pair where outer appears before inner in sorted order. If outer and inner have same L, outer with larger R sorted first, detected. Good.
Need also detect duplicate equal intervals: sorted by L,R desc; first duplicate contains second, detected.

Union covers U:
Need exists two intervals with L=1 and R=N (not necessarily distinct? distinct; full handled). If there is at least one interval with L=1 and at least one interval with R=N, and they are distinct, then union covers U. If the same interval has both, full cost1 handled. Now, let's revisit the theorem that q=2 nonempty dominated by q=1. We used to restrict to cost2 disjoint and cost3 q=1. Need ensure no optimal solution with q=2 nonempty and cost >3 but less than our computed? We argued any q=2 nonempty can be converted to q=1 with cost ≤ q=2. Let's formalize for final explanation maybe not needed in code, but for confidence.

Given q=2 solution with type2 intervals X,Y, B=X∩Y nonempty, type1 set P covers B, cost 2+|P|. Need show there is q=1 solution cost ≤2+|P|. We provided proof. Let's refine for all cases:
- If X and Y are such that one contains the other, say X⊆Y. Then B=X. P covers X. Choose type2 X, type1 P: cost 1+|P| ≤2+|P|.
- Else let X=[a,b], Y=[c,d] with a<c≤b<d (after ordering by L; if overlap nonempty and neither contains, this holds). B=[c,b]. Let A=∪P.
If A contains X or Y, choose type2 that interval and type1 P: cost 1+|P|.
Otherwise A contains neither. Since A covers B, point c is covered by some C∈P. If C⊆X∪Y=[a,d], then choose type2 C, type1 (P\{C})∪{X,Y}; cost 1+(|P|-1)+2 = |P|+2, equal. If C not subset X∪Y:
   - If R_C>d, then because C covers c (L_C≤c≤R_C), C contains Y? Need C contains Y=[c,d] if L_C≤c and R_C≥d. R_C>d, L_C≤c, yes. Then A contains Y, contradiction.
   - If L_C<a, then C covers [a,c-1] (since L_C<a and R_C≥c). Since A covers B=[c,b], A covers X=[a,b], contradiction.
   - If both L_C<a and R_C>d, both contradictions. Thus C⊆X∪Y. So q=1 exists. Good.
If X,Y disjoint, B empty, q=2 cost2; this is covered by disjoint check. If B empty, no type1 needed. So q=2 nonempty never strictly better. Good.

Now, what about q=0 with cost >2 in common-point case? We said if no disjoint, all intervals contain p, union of any subset is contiguous, so cover U iff min L=1 and max R=N, which can be done with at most 2 intervals. Thus q=0 cost >2 impossible. If disjoint exists, answer≤2. So no need compute q=0 >2. This is a big simplification. Need be absolutely sure: If no disjoint, all intervals contain p. Any subset union is contiguous [min L, max R] because all intervals contain p. To cover U=[1,N], need min L=1 and max R=N. If there is an interval with L=1 and another with R=N, two intervals suffice. If the same interval full, one. If no such, no subset can cover U. Thus q=0 cost cannot be >2. Great.

Similarly q=1 cost in no-disjoint case: For any type2 interval T, to cover T by type1 intervals P, all intervals in P contain p, so union of P is contiguous [min L, max R]. Need min L≤L_T and max R≥R_T. If both conditions hold, two intervals suffice (one min L, one max R). If one interval contains T, one suffices. If not, impossible. Thus q=1 cost ≤3 or impossible. Great.

Therefore the entire problem reduces to checking costs 1,2,3, else -1. This is surprisingly simple. Need ensure cost2 via union U is included in no-disjoint case; but if no disjoint, union U cost2 can happen. We check.

Let's test with a random brute force for small N/M mentally to ensure no missing cost2/3.

Let's enumerate all possible solutions for M=2:
- Cost1 if full interval.
- Cost2 if:
  * both type1 cover U
  * one type1 one type2 containment
  * both type2 disjoint
- Cost3 impossible with M=2 (max cost2). If none, -1.
Our algorithm: after cost1, cost2 checks. If no cost2, cost3 condition with M=2? Suppose no cost2 but cost3 condition true? For M=2, cost3 would require two type1 intervals plus one type2 = all 3 ops, but M=2 impossible. Our cost3 condition uses minL_excl and maxR_excl. With M=2, for T=interval0, minL_excl = L1, maxR_excl = R1. Condition L1≤L0 and R1≥R0 means interval1 contains interval0 -> cost2, which would have been caught by containment. So cost3 condition won't be true if cost2 absent. For T=interval1 similarly. Thus no false cost3. Good.

M=3, cost3 possible. Our algorithm outputs cost3 if condition true. Need ensure there are indeed 3 distinct ops: type2 T, type1 A (minL_excl), type1 B (maxR_excl). If A or B equals T? Excluded. If A==B? Then containment cost2 would have been caught. But what if A==B and containment check failed due to sorting bug? We'll implement robust. If A==B, outputting cost3 with same interval twice invalid. But as argued, A==B means that interval has L≤L_T and R≥R_T, so contains T. Containment check would find it. Let's ensure containment check finds any pair i,j with L_i≤L_j and R_i≥R_j. If A==B=J, J contains T. Sorting by L asc, R desc. If J.L < T.L, J processed before T, maxR≥J.R≥T.R, detected when processing T. If J.L == T.L and J.R > T.R, J before T due to R desc, detected. If J.L == T.L and J.R == T.R (duplicate), J before T, detected. If J.L > T.L, cannot contain T. So yes. Thus A!=B.

But wait: Cost3 condition uses minL_excl and maxR_excl. Suppose minL_excl interval A has L≤L_T but does NOT contain p? In no-disjoint case all contain p. If no disjoint check passed, all intervals pairwise intersect, so all contain p. Good. If there are disjoint intervals, we would have returned cost2 before cost3. So no issue.

Now, let's think about the cost2 disjoint detection with intervals that touch? Disjoint means no common integer: R_i < L_j or R_j < L_i. If R_i +1 = L_j, they are disjoint (no overlap). Example [1,2] and [3,5] disjoint. Sweep condition max_R < L_i (strict) catches R=2 < L=3. Good. If R_i = L_i? intervals [1,1] and [2,2] disjoint. Good.

Containment detection with equal intervals: If two identical intervals, cost2 via type2 one, type1 other. Our containment sweep sorted by L asc, R desc. For equal intervals, first has same R, maxR >= R, detects. Good.

Union U detection: has_L1 and has_RN. If the same interval full, cost1 returned. If there are two intervals one L=1 and one R=N but they are the same? impossible unless full. If full exists, cost1. So after cost1, if both flags true, distinct. But what if there are two intervals, one L=1 and R=N? full, cost1. So okay.

Now, let's consider a case where cost2 via union U but also disjoint? Example [1,2],[3,5] disjoint and union U. Our disjoint check returns type2 both. Valid. If we check union first, type1 both also valid. Both cost2. Good.

Now, what about cost2 via containment and disjoint? Example [1,5] full cost1. Non-full containment cannot be disjoint because inner subset outer, so intersect. So no conflict.

Now, let's attempt to prove that if no cost2, cost3 condition is necessary and sufficient for achievability. We already did. Need also output if cost3 condition false -> -1. Is it possible to achieve with cost >3 in no-disjoint/no-cost2? We argued no: q=0 impossible or cost2; q=1 cost≤3 or impossible; q=2 nonempty dominated; q=2 disjoint would be cost2. So no. Thus -1.

Let's test with a tricky case: N=5, intervals all contain p=3, no cost2, cost3 condition true. Example [1,3],[3,5],[2,4]? Wait [1,3] and [3,5] intersect at3, [2,4] contains? [2,4] not contain [1,3] (1 missing), not contain [3,5] (5 missing). No full. Union U? L=1 yes, R=5 yes -> cost2 via union [1,3]∪[3,5] = [1,5]. So output2. Good.

Need no union U: N=6, intervals [1,3],[3,5],[2,4]? R=5 not6, L=1 yes, no R=6. No containment? [1,3] and [3,5] intersect; [2,4] not contain. Cost3 condition:

What if cost3 condition true but T is the global minL interval? Then minL_excl is second min. Condition second_min ≤ L_T. If T has smallest L, this requires another interval with L≤L_T, i.e., same L. That would be duplicate/equal L. If no containment, maybe possible. Example intervals [2,5], [2,6]? containment [2,6] contains [2,5] -> cost2. So if T has global min L, another interval with same L and R≥R_T would contain T, cost2. If no cost2, second_min > L_T, condition false. Thus T cannot be global min. Similarly T cannot be global max R. So in cost3, T is not extreme; minL_excl and maxR_excl are from other intervals. Good.

Let's test a no-cost2 case where cost3 condition true:

Now, let's consider if there is a solution with cost3 via q=0 (three type1 intervals cover U) in a case with disjoint? But if disjoint exists, cost2. If no disjoint, q=0 >2 impossible. So no.

Now, let's think about the output format. Need print K then op list. For cost1:

Now, let's design data structures for min/max excluding.

We have arrays L[0..M-1], R[0..M-1].

Find:
min1_val = min L, min1_idx = first index with that L.
min2_val = second smallest L (could INF if none). Need for each i:
if i == min1_idx: minL_excl = min2_val else minL_excl = min1_val.
But what if multiple intervals have the same minimum L? min1_idx is one of them. For another interval with same min L, minL_excl should be min1_val (because excluding it, the other min remains). For min1_idx itself, minL_excl = min2_val (which may also equal min1_val if duplicate). So we need second smallest value, not necessarily distinct index. We can compute by scanning:
min1_val = INF, min1_idx=-1, min2_val=INF.
For each i:
 if L[i] < min1_val:
   min2_val = min1_val
   min1_val = L[i]; min1_idx=i
 elif L[i] < min2_val:
   min2_val = L[i]
This gives min2_val = smallest L among indices excluding min1_idx? If duplicate min, when second duplicate processed, L == min1_val, so elif L < min2_val (if min2_val INF) sets min2_val = min1_val. Good. For min1_idx, minL_excl = min2_val. For others, minL_excl = min1_val. Similarly for max:
max1_val = -INF, max1_idx=-1, max2_val=-INF.
if R[i] > max1_val:
  max2_val = max1_val
  max1_val = R[i]; max1_idx=i
elif R[i] > max2_val:
  max2_val = R[i]
For max1_idx, maxR_excl = max2_val; else maxR_excl = max1_val.

Cost3 condition:
for i in range(M):
  minL_excl = min2_val if i==min1_idx else min1_val
  maxR_excl = max2_val if i==max1_idx else max1_val
  if minL_excl <= L[i] and maxR_excl >= R[i]:
      found.
Need also ensure there exist at least two intervals? If M=1, min2_val=INF, max2_val=-INF, condition false. Good.

But wait: If i is not min1_idx, minL_excl = min1_val. If min1_idx == i? no. If i is max1_idx, maxR_excl = max2_val. If i is both min1_idx and max1_idx (the same interval has global min L and max R), then for that i, minL_excl=min2, maxR_excl=max2. For other intervals, minL_excl=min1, maxR_excl=max1. Could condition hold for i itself? Need min2≤L_i and max2≥R_i. If i has min L and max R, L_i is smallest, so min2≤L_i only if duplicate min; max2≥R_i only if duplicate max. If duplicates, maybe. But if i is extreme, likely not. Anyway.

Now, cost2 detection details.

Let's implement functions to find disjoint pair, containment pair, union pair.

Disjoint pair:
Create list of tuples (L,R,idx). Sort by L.
maxR = -1, maxIdx = -1
for (l,r,idx) in sorted:
    if maxR < l:  # previous interval with maxR ends before current starts
        # disjoint pair (maxIdx, idx)
        return maxIdx, idx
    if r > maxR:
        maxR = r; maxIdx = idx
But is it sufficient to check only maxR? Suppose maxR < l, then the interval with maxR is disjoint from current. Yes. If maxR >= l, current intersects the interval with maxR, but there might be another previous interval disjoint? Since maxR is the maximum end among previous; if maxR >= l, every previous interval has R ≤ maxR, but some could have R < l? Wait maxR >= l doesn't mean all previous R >= l. There could be a previous interval with small R < l disjoint from current, even though maxR >= l from another interval. Example intervals: [1,10] (maxR=10), [2,3], current [4,5]. Sorted by L: [1,10], [2,3], [4,5]. At current [4,5], maxR=10 >=4, so our check misses disjoint pair [2,3] and [4,5]. But [1,10] intersects both, and [2,3] disjoint from [4,5]. Need detect any disjoint pair, not necessarily with maxR. The standard sweep for "exists pair of disjoint intervals" can be done differently: sort by L, track maxR. If maxR < l, then the interval with maxR is disjoint. But if maxR >= l, there may still be disjoint pair with a non-max previous interval. However if maxR >= l, does that imply no disjoint pair? No, as example shows [2,3] and [4,5] disjoint while maxR=10. But wait [1,10] intersects current and [2,3]; intervals [2,3] and [4,5] are disjoint, so disjoint exists. Our sweep with maxR would miss. Need fix.

We need detect if there exist two intervals with R_i < L_j (after sorting by L). This is equivalent to: in sorted by L, is there a gap between the maximum R among intervals up to some point and the next L? Actually if any previous interval has R < current L, then disjoint. The maximum R being ≥ current L doesn't preclude another previous interval with R < current L. But if maxR ≥ current L, the interval with maxR overlaps current, but a smaller previous interval may be disjoint. So we need know if there exists any previous interval with R < current L, i.e., the minimum R among previous? Wait if any previous R < current L. Since current L is fixed, we need min R among previous < current L. If the minimum R among previous < current L, then that interval is disjoint from current. So track minR_prev, not maxR. But is minR_prev enough? Example previous intervals [1,10] R=10, [2,3] R=3, current L=4. minR_prev=3<4, disjoint. Yes. If minR_prev < current L, disjoint. If minR_prev >= current L, then all previous intervals have R ≥ current L, so all intersect current. But there could be disjoint pair among previous intervals themselves, which would have been detected earlier when the later of those two was processed. So sweep tracking minR_prev works:
Sort by L ascending. Maintain minR_prev = minimum R among previous intervals. For current interval with L=l, if minR_prev < l, then the previous interval with minR_prev is disjoint from current. Return pair. If not, update minR_prev = min(minR_prev, r).
Is this correct? Let's test with intervals [1,10], [2,3], [4,5]:
Sorted [1,10], [2,3], [4,5].
minR_prev after first =10.
Current [2,3]: minR_prev=10 <2? no. update min=3.
Current [4,5]: minR_prev=3 <4 yes -> disjoint [2,3] and [4,5]. Good.
What about intervals [1,2], [3,10] sorted: minR_prev=2, current L=3, 2<3 -> disjoint. Good.
What about intervals [1,5], [2,3], [4,6]: minR_prev after first=5; current [2,3] 5<2 no, min=3; current [4,6] 3<4 yes -> [2,3] and [4,6] disjoint? [2,3] and [4,6] are disjoint (3<4), yes. Even though [1,5] overlaps both. Good.
So disjoint detection: sort by L, track min R among previous. If minR_prev < L_current, disjoint. Need store index of interval with minR_prev. But if multiple, any. When updating minR, update idx.

Wait, is minR_prev sufficient? Suppose previous intervals R values: 10, 3. min=3. If current L=4, disjoint with R=3. Yes. If current L=2, min=3 not <2, no previous disjoint with current (R=3≥2, R=10≥2). Good. If current L=3, min=3 not <3, intervals touch at 3? R=3 and L=3 intersect at 3, not disjoint. Correct. So yes.

But what if disjoint pair appears with current interval having larger L but previous interval's R < current L, minR_prev will catch. If the disjoint pair's previous interval is not the minR? If any R < L, then minR ≤ that R < L, so minR catches. Good.

Thus disjoint check:
sort by L ascending.
minR = INF, minIdx=-1
for l,r,idx in sorted:

Containment detection:

maxR = -1, maxIdx = -1
for l,r,idx in sorted:
    if maxR >= r:
        # maxIdx interval contains current (since maxIdx L <= l? Need ensure maxIdx L <= l. Because sorted by L, all previous have L <= current L. If equal L, previous with larger R sorted before; still L<=. So yes.)

But is this sufficient to detect any containment where outer appears after inner? Sorting by L ascending ensures outer with smaller L appears before inner. If outer and inner have same L, outer with larger R appears before inner due to R descending. So yes. What if outer has L smaller but R smaller? Then outer doesn't contain inner. maxR may be from some other interval. If maxR >= r, that maxR interval contains current. Good.
Need ensure maxIdx interval is not current. Since check before update, maxIdx from previous. Good.
If equal intervals, first has same R, maxR >= r, returns first as outer, second as inner. Good.

If idxL != -1 and idxR != -1:
   if idxL == idxR: full interval, cost1 already returned. But if full interval exists, cost1 returned. So here idxL != idxR. However if full interval exists but we didn't return? We will return cost1 first. So no.
Return idxL, idxR.

But wait: What if there is an interval with L=1 and R=N (full) but we are checking cost2 after cost1? We'll return cost1 before. Good.

Now, order of cost2 checks: If disjoint exists, output type2 both. If containment exists, output type2 inner/type1 outer. If union exists, output type1 both. But consider a case where disjoint exists and also full? full returned. If disjoint exists, cost2. Good.

Could there be a cost2 solution via containment but our containment check returns a pair where outer interval is also one of the type2 intervals in a disjoint pair? Doesn't matter.

Now, let's think about output for cost2 via disjoint: We have two intervals with R_i < L_j (or R_j < L_i). Our disjoint detection returns minIdx (previous) and current idx with R_min < L_current. So type2 for both. Need set ops[minIdx]=2, ops[idx]=2. Valid.

Cost2 via containment: outer = maxIdx, inner = idx. Set ops[outer]=1, ops[inner]=2. Valid. If outer and inner distinct. Good.

Cost2 via union: idxL, idxR. Set ops[idxL]=1, ops[idxR]=1. Valid. If idxL==idxR (full) cost1; but if full exists, cost1. If somehow idxL==idxR and no full? impossible. We can guard.

Now, let's think about the "no disjoint" check. If disjoint exists, we return cost2. But what if disjoint exists and also cost1 full? cost1 returned first. Good.

Now, let's verify with a brute-force mental for small N to ensure no cost4 cases. Suppose intervals all contain p, no cost2, cost3 condition false. Could there be a solution with q=1 using 3 type1 intervals to cover T even though minL_excl > L_T or maxR_excl < R_T? Since all intervals contain p, union of any set is [min L, max R]. If minL_excl > L_T, no interval other than T starts at or before L_T, so no type1 interval can cover L_T. Thus impossible. If maxR_excl < R_T, no interval other than T reaches R_T. Thus impossible. So no. q=0 impossible. q=2 nonempty dominated. So -1. Good.

Now, let's revisit the reduction from q=2 nonempty to q=1 in the context of our simplified cost3. We don't need to implement q=2 nonempty. But need be certain that q=2 nonempty with cost maybe 3 could be the only solution when cost3 condition false? We proved it can be converted to q=1 with cost ≤ q=2. If q=2 cost=3, q=1 cost≤3. If q=1 cost≤3, our cost3 condition should detect it. Let's test with a q=2 nonempty solution that is not captured by our cost2/cost3? Example earlier X=[1,100], Y=[50,149], P={C=[40,110]} q=2 cost3. Convert to q=1? We found q=1 type2 C with type1 X,Y cost3. Our cost3 condition: intervals X,Y,C. No disjoint? X and Y overlap, all contain p=50? X [1,100], Y[50,149], C[40,110] all contain50. No full, no cost2? Check containment: C [40,110] contains? X [1,100] not (1<40), Y [50,149] not (149>110). X and Y no. Union U? L=1 (X), R=149 not N if N=200. No. Cost3 condition:
Global minL=1 (X), maxR=149 (Y).
For T=C [40,110]: minL_excl=1≤40, maxR_excl=149≥110 -> cost3. Output type2 C, type1 X,Y. Valid. Good.

What if q=2 nonempty with P size 2, q=2 cost4, conversion q=1 cost maybe 4. Our cost3 condition would detect cost3? Wait if q=1 cost = 1 + cover_excl. In no-disjoint, cover_excl is at most 2 if possible. So q=1 cost ≤3. Thus if q=2 cost4, there is q=1 cost≤3? Our proof said q=1 cost ≤ q=2 cost =4, but in no-disjoint case q=1 cost cannot be 4 because two intervals suffice. Actually if q=1 possible, cover_excl≤2, so cost≤3. Thus q=2 nonempty with cost≥4 would imply q=1 cost≤3, so answer≤3. Our algorithm would find cost3. Good.

Let's test a q=2 nonempty with P size2 where q=1 conversion maybe uses C contained in X∪Y and cost = k+2 =4? But in no-disjoint, if C contained in X∪Y, then q=1 type2 C with type1 X,Y cost3 (k=1? Wait P size2, C∈P, q=1 cost =1+(|P|-1)+2 = |P|+2 =4. But maybe we can do better? Let's see. Now, let's ensure cost3 condition doesn't require T to be an input interval? It does; we check each interval. In q=1, type2 must be an input interval. Good.

Now, let's consider if there are intervals with L=1 and R=N but no full? impossible. Cost1 check.

Now, let's think about memory and input parsing. N up to 1e6, M 2e5. Store L,R lists of length M. Output ops list of ints length M. Use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() for speed.

Implementation plan:

def solve():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    L = [0]*M; R=[0]*M
    idx=2
    for i in range(M):
        L[i]=data[idx]; R[i]=data[idx+1]; idx+=2

    ops = [0]*M

    # cost1
    full_idx = -1
    for i in range(M):
        if L[i]==1 and R[i]==N:
            full_idx=i; break
    if full_idx != -1:
        ops[full_idx]=1
        print(1)
        print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
        return

    # cost2 checks

    # disjoint
    order = sorted(range(M), key=lambda i: (L[i], R[i]))  # R tie not important
    minR = N+1  # INF
    minIdx = -1
    for i in order:
        if minR < L[i]:
            # disjoint minIdx, i
            ops[minIdx]=2; ops[i]=2
            print(2)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return
        if R[i] < minR:
            minR = R[i]; minIdx = i
    # Wait update order: For first interval, minR INF, check false, update. Good.
    # But consider sorted by L; if minR < L[i], the interval with minR is previous. However, what if minIdx interval has L > L[i]? No, previous sorted by L so L<=L[i]. Good.
    # Is minR tracking minimum R among all previous. If minR < L[i], that previous interval ends before current starts. Disjoint. Good.

    # containment
    order = sorted(range(M), key=lambda i: (L[i], -R[i]))
    maxR = -1
    maxIdx = -1
    for i in order:
        if maxR >= R[i]:
            # maxIdx contains i
            ops[maxIdx]=1; ops[i]=2
            print(2)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return
        if R[i] > maxR:
            maxR = R[i]; maxIdx = i

    # union U
    idxL = -1; idxR = -1
    for i in range(M):
        if L[i]==1 and idxL==-1:
            idxL=i
        if R[i]==N and idxR==-1:
            idxR=i
    if idxL != -1 and idxR != -1:
        # full would have returned, so idxL != idxR
        ops[idxL]=1; ops[idxR]=1
        print(2)
        print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
        return

    # cost3
    # compute min1, min2, max1, max2
    INF = 10**18
    min1_val = INF; min1_idx = -1; min2_val = INF; min2_idx = -1
    max1_val = -INF; max1_idx = -1; max2_val = -INF; max2_idx = -1
    for i in range(M):
        l = L[i]; r = R[i]
        if l < min1_val:
            min2_val = min1_val; min2_idx = min1_idx
            min1_val = l; min1_idx = i
        elif l < min2_val:
            min2_val = l; min2_idx = i
        if r > max1_val:
            max2_val = max1_val; max2_idx = max1_idx
            max1_val = r; max1_idx = i
        elif r > max2_val:
            max2_val = r; max2_idx = i

    for i in range(M):
        minL_excl = min2_val if i == min1_idx else min1_val
        maxR_excl = max2_val if i == max1_idx else max1_val
        if minL_excl <= L[i] and maxR_excl >= R[i]:
            a = min2_idx if i == min1_idx else min1_idx
            b = max2_idx if i == max1_idx else max1_idx
            if a == -1 or b == -1:
                continue
            if a == b:
                # fallback cost2
                ops[i]=2; ops[a]=1
                print(2)
                print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
                return
            ops[i]=2; ops[a]=1; ops[b]=1
            print(3)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return

    print(-1)

Now, let's think about if there is a case with M=3, no disjoint, no containment, union U false, but min L interval and max R interval cover the third,
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    L = [0] * M
    R = [0] * M
    p = 2
    for i in range(M):
        L[i] = data[p]
        R[i] = data[p + 1]
        p += 2

    ops = [0] * M

    # Cost 1: one operation of type 1 with [1, N]
    full = -1
    for i in range(M):
        if L[i] == 1 and R[i] == N:
            full = i
            break
    if full != -1:
        ops[full] = 1
        print(1)
        print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
        return

    # Cost 2 check 1: two disjoint intervals -> both type 2
    order = sorted(range(M), key=lambda i: (L[i], R[i]))
    min_r = N + 1
    min_idx = -1
    for i in order:
        if min_r < L[i]:
            ops[min_idx] = 2
            ops[i] = 2
            print(2)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return
        if R[i] < min_r:
            min_r = R[i]
            min_idx = i

    # Cost 2 check 2: one interval contains another -> outer type 1, inner type 2
    order = sorted(range(M), key=lambda i: (L[i], -R[i]))
    max_r = -1
    max_idx = -1
    for i in order:
        if max_r >= R[i]:
            ops[max_idx] = 1
            ops[i] = 2
            print(2)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return
        if R[i] > max_r:
            max_r = R[i]
            max_idx = i

    # Cost 2 check 3: two intervals cover [1, N] -> both type 1
    idx_l = -1
    idx_r = -1
    for i in range(M):
        if L[i] == 1 and idx_l == -1:
            idx_l = i
        if R[i] == N and idx_r == -1:
            idx_r = i
    if idx_l != -1 and idx_r != -1:
        if idx_l == idx_r:
            ops[idx_l] = 1
            print(1)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return
        ops[idx_l] = 1
        ops[idx_r] = 1
        print(2)
        print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
        return

    # Cost 3: choose one interval as type 2,
    # and cover it by the globally leftmost and rightmost other intervals.
    INF = 10**18
    min1_val, min1_idx = INF, -1
    min2_val, min2_idx = INF, -1
    max1_val, max1_idx = -INF, -1
    max2_val, max2_idx = -INF, -1

    for i in range(M):
        l, r = L[i], R[i]

        if l < min1_val:
            min2_val, min2_idx = min1_val, min1_idx
            min1_val, min1_idx = l, i
        elif l < min2_val:
            min2_val, min2_idx = l, i

        if r > max1_val:
            max2_val, max2_idx = max1_val, max1_idx
            max1_val, max1_idx = r, i
        elif r > max2_val:
            max2_val, max2_idx = r, i

    for i in range(M):
        min_l_excl = min2_val if i == min1_idx else min1_val
        max_r_excl = max2_val if i == max1_idx else max1_val

        if min_l_excl <= L[i] and max_r_excl >= R[i]:
            a = min2_idx if i == min1_idx else min1_idx
            b = max2_idx if i == max1_idx else max1_idx

            if a == -1 or b == -1:
                continue

            # This should not happen after the cost-2 checks, but keep it safe.
            if a == b:
                ops[i] = 2
                ops[a] = 1
                print(2)
                print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
                return

            ops[i] = 2
            ops[a] = 1
            ops[b] = 1
            print(3)
            print(' '.join(map(str, ops)))
            return

    print(-1)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
